height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$5$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to15.39995pt{\vss\hbox to15.00002pt{\hss$6$\hss}\vss}\kern-15.39995pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\vrule height=15.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-15.39995pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 15.00002pt\hrule width=15.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr}}\kern 882.43542pt}
.
We denote the elements
\mathbf{a}_{T_{\lambda}}

and
\mathbf{b}_{T_{\lambda}}
of
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}
and

\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}
. Thus,
[
EGHLSVY11
, Theorem 5.12.2]
or

[
Grinbe25
, Proposition 5.11.19
(c)
]
shows that
S^{\lambda}\cong\mathcal{A}\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}
as left

\mathcal{A}
-modules
14
14

14

Specifically, this follows from
[
EGHLSVY11
, Theorem
5.12.2]
after observing that the
a_{\lambda}
and
b_{\lambda}
in

[
EGHLSVY11
, Theorem 5.12.2]
are our
\dfrac{1}{\left|\mathcal{R}\left(T\right)\right|}\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}
and
\dfrac{1}{\left|\mathcal{C}\left(T\right)\right|}\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}
(the fractions
here are well-defined, since
\left|\mathcal{R}\left(T\right)\right|
and
\left|\mathcal{C}\left(T\right)\right|
are
divisors of
n!
and thus invertible in
\mathbf{k}
). Alternatively, this
follows by applying
[
Grinbe25
, Theorem 5.5.13
(b)
]
to
T=T_{\lambda}

and noticing that
\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{a}_{T}\mathbf{b}_{T}=\nabla_{\operatorname*{Row}T}\nabla_{\operatorname*{Col}T}^{-}=\mathbf{F}_{T}
in the notations of
[
Grinbe25
, Theorem 5.5.13
(b)
]
and that the number
h^{\lambda}
defined in
[
Grinbe25
, Theorem 5.5.13

(b)
]
is invertible in
\mathbf{k}
(since it is a divisor of

n!
).
.

Hence,
S^{\lambda}\cong\underbrace{\mathcal{A}\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}}_{\subseteq\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}\subseteq\mathcal{A}\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}
. It thus suffices to show that
\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\mathcal{A}\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}=0
(since
\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}S^{\lambda}=0
will then follow). In other words, it suffices to
show that
\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}w\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}=0
for any

w\in S_{n}
. But this is not hard:

Let
w\in S_{n}
. Let
T
be the Young tableau of shape
\lambda
obtained
from
T_{\lambda}
by applying the permutation
w
to each entry. (This is

w\rightharpoonup T_{\lambda}
in the notations of
[
Grinbe25
, Definition
5.3.9]
.) Thus,
\mathbf{b}_{T}=w\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}w^{-1}
(by

[
Grinbe25
, Proposition 5.5.11]
, applied to
T_{\lambda}
instead of
T
), so
that
w\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{b}_{T}w
.

The first column of the tableau
T
contains
\ell\left(\lambda\right)

entries, and thus contains more than
k
entries (since
\ell\left(\lambda\right)>k
). Hence, at least two entries of this column belong to the
same block of
\mathbf{A}
(by the pigeonhole principle, since
\mathbf{A}

has only
\ell\left(\mathbf{A}\right)\leq k
blocks). Pick two such
entries. Let
\tau\in S_{n}
be the transposition that swaps these two
entries. This transposition
\tau
thus preserves the blocks of
\mathbf{A}

(since it swaps two numbers from the same block), and therefore preserves

\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}
from the right (i.e., satisfies

\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\tau=\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}
) because
of (
20
). On the other hand, this transposition
\tau
swaps two
entries in the first column of
T
, and thus belongs to the column group

\mathcal{C}\left(T\right)
of
T
. Hence,
\mathbf{b}_{T}=\left(1-\tau\right)\eta
for some
\eta\in\mathcal{A}
(since
\left\langle\tau\right\rangle=\left\{1,\tau\right\}
is a subgroup of the column
group
\mathcal{C}\left(T\right)
, and since the permutation
\tau
is
odd; see
[
Grinbe25
, Proposition 5.5.9
(b)
]
for details). Thus,

\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\underbrace{w\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}}_{=\mathbf{b}_{T}w}=\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\underbrace{\mathbf{b}_{T}}_{=\left(1-\tau\right)\eta}w=\underbrace{\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\left(1-\tau\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}-\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\tau\\
=0\\
\text{(since }\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\tau=\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\eta w=0.

This completes our proof of
\nabla_{\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}}S^{\lambda}=0
.
Thus, as explained above, we have proved Claim 1.

Proof 2.80

(Proof of Claim 2.)
.

This is fairly similar to the above proof of Claim 1. Here
are the main milestones of the proof:

Let
\lambda\vdash n
be a partition that satisfies
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k
. We must prove that all
a\in\mathcal{J}_{k}
satisfy

aS^{\lambda}=0
.

Recall that
\mathcal{J}_{k}=\mathcal{A}\cdot\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{U}^{-}\ \mid\ U\text{ is a subset of }\left[n\right]\text{ having
size }k+1\right\}
(by Proposition
2.14

(b)
). Hence, it
suffices to show that
\nabla_{U}^{-}S^{\lambda}=0
whenever
U
is a subset
of
\left[n\right]
having size
k+1
(because then,
\mathcal{A}\underbrace{\nabla_{U}^{-}S^{\lambda}}_{=0}=0
will automatically follow).

So let us fix a subset
U
of
\left[n\right]
having size
k+1
. Consider
the Young tableau
T_{\lambda}
and the elements
\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}
and

\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}
defined as in the above proof of Claim 1. Then,

S^{\lambda}\cong\mathcal{A}\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}\mathbf{b}_{\lambda}
. Hence,
in order to prove that
\nabla_{U}^{-}S^{\lambda}=0
, it suffices to show that

\nabla_{U}^{-}\mathcal{A}\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}=0
. For this, in turn, it
suffices to show that
\nabla_{U}^{-}w\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}=0
for each
w\in S_{n}
.

So let
w\in S_{n}
be arbitrary. Let
T
be the Young tableau of shape

\lambda
obtained from
T_{\lambda}
by applying the permutation
w
to each
entry. Then,
\mathbf{a}_{T}=w\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}w^{-1}
(by

[
Grinbe25
, Proposition 5.5.11]
), so that
w\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{a}_{T}w
.

But the pigeonhole principle shows that there are two distinct elements of
U

that belong to the same row of our tableau
T
(since the set
U
has
k+1

elements, but the tableau
T
has only
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k

rows). Let
\tau
be the transposition that swaps these two elements. Then,

\nabla_{U}^{-}\tau=-\nabla_{U}^{-}
(by
[
Grinbe25
, Proposition 3.7.4
(c)
]
), but also
\mathbf{a}_{T}=\left(1+\tau\right)\eta
for some

\eta\in\mathcal{A}
(since
\left\langle\tau\right\rangle=\left\{1,\tau\right\}
is a subgroup of the row group
\mathcal{R}\left(T\right)
; see
[
Grinbe25
, Proposition 5.5.8
(b)
]
for details). Thus,

\nabla_{U}^{-}\underbrace{w\mathbf{a}_{\lambda}}_{=\mathbf{a}_{T}w}=\nabla_{U}^{-}\underbrace{\mathbf{a}_{T}}_{=\left(1+\tau\right)\eta}w=\underbrace{\nabla_{U}^{-}\left(1+\tau\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\nabla_{U}^{-}+\nabla_{U}^{-}\tau\\
=0\\
\text{(since }\nabla_{U}^{-}\tau=-\nabla_{U}^{-}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\eta w=0.

As we explained above, this completes the proof of Claim 2.

However, if
X
and
Y
are two disjoint subsets of
\left\{\lambda\ \mid\ \lambda\vdash n\right\}
, then
\mathcal{A}_{X}\cap\mathcal{A}_{Y}=0
(since the subproducts
\prod\limits\limits_{\lambda\in X}\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)
and
\prod\limits\limits_{\lambda\in Y}\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)
of
\prod\limits\limits_{\lambda\vdash n}\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)
have intersection

0
). Thus,

\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\right\}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)>k\right\}}=0.

Meanwhile, Claim 1 and Claim 2 yield

\mathcal{I}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\right\}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{J}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)>k\right\}}.

Furthermore, Theorem
2.17

(g)
yields
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{I}_{k}\oplus\mathcal{J}_{k}
(internal direct sum), so that

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{I}_{k}+\mathcal{J}_{k}
. Thus, Lemma
2.76

(applied to
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}_{k}
and

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}_{k}
and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\right\}}
and

\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)>k\right\}}
) yields

\mathcal{I}_{k}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\right\}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{J}_{k}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)>k\right\}}.

This proves Theorem
2.75
.

Note that part of Theorem
2.75
for
k=3
appears in

[
BCEHK23
, Proposition 3.5 (a)]
. Moreover,
[
KlStVo25
, Theorem 2.2]

can also be viewed as a corollary of Theorem
2.75
(indeed, we
already saw that the
k
-swap algebra from
[
KlStVo25
, Theorem 2.2]
is
isomorphic to our
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{J}_{k}
, which according to Theorem

2.75
is in turn isomorphic to
\prod\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)
).

2.9.2
Application: Counting avoiding permutations

As a consequence of Theorem
2.75
, we recover a classical
enumerative result that is commonly proved using the RSK correspondence (see,
e.g.,
[
Bona22
, (7.2)]
,
[
Stanle23
, Corollary 7.23.12]
):

Corollary 2.81
.

For each partition
\lambda
of
n
, let
f^{\lambda}
be
the number of standard tableaux of shape
\lambda
. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then, the number of all permutations
w\in S_{n}
that avoid
12\cdots\left(k+1\right)
is
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}=\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\lambda_{1}\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
.

Proof 2.82
.

Let
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Q}
. Let us use the notations of Theorem

2.75
. Then, the standard basis theorem for Specht modules (see,
e.g.,
[
Grinbe25
, Lemma 5.9.17]
) says that

\dim\left(S^{\lambda}\right)=f^{\lambda}

(75)

for each partition
\lambda
of
n
.

However, Theorem
2.75
shows that

\mathcal{I}_{k}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\right\}}\cong\prod\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{as }\mathbf{k}\text{-vector spaces}

(since
\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\right\}}
was defined as the preimage of

\prod\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)
under the isomorphism

\operatorname*{AW}
). Thus,

\dim\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)=\dim\left(\prod\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)\right)=\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\ \ \underbrace{\dim\left(\operatorname*{End}\left(S^{\lambda}\right)\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left(\dim\left(S^{\lambda}\right)\right)^{2}=\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.cor.num-avoid.dimSlam}))}\end{subarray}}=\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}.

On the other hand, Theorem
2.17

(c)
yields

\dim\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)=\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\right|.

Comparing these two equalities, we find
\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\right|=\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
. In other words, the number of all permutations
w\in S_{n}
that avoid

12\cdots\left(k+1\right)
is
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
.

It remains to prove that this sum also equals
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\lambda_{1}\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
. But this is
easy: The bijection
\left\{\lambda\vdash n\right\}\rightarrow\left\{\lambda\vdash n\right\}
that sends each partition
\lambda
to its
transpose
\lambda^{t}
swaps the roles of
\ell\left(\lambda\right)
and

\lambda_{1}
(that is, we have
\ell\left(\lambda^{t}\right)=\lambda_{1}

and
\lambda_{1}^{t}=\ell\left(\lambda\right)
). Thus, the sums

\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
and
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\lambda_{1}\leq k\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
are the same up to
reindexing using this bijection. Hence, Corollary
2.81
is proved.

2.9.3
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)

Corollary
2.81
is not the end of the line. There is a more
general result (
[
Schens60
, Theorem 3]
,
[
Stanle71
, Proposition 17.5]
)
saying the following:

Theorem 2.83
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the number of all
permutations
w\in S_{n}
that avoid
12\cdots\left(k+1\right)
and

\left(\ell+1\right)\ell\cdots 1
is
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\text{ and }\lambda_{1}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
.

Proof 2.84
.

From
[
Schens60
, Theorem 3]
or
[
Stanle71
, Proposition 17.5]
, we know
that for any
c,d\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\text{the number of permutations }w\in S_{n}\text{ such
that}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{the longest increasing subsequence of
}w\text{ has length }c\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{and the longest decreasing subsequence of
}w\text{ has length }d\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)=d\text{ and }\lambda_{1}=c\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}.

Summing this over all
c\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,\ell\right\}
and all

d\in\left\{0,1,\ldots,k\right\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\text{the number of permutations }w\in S_{n}\text{ such
that}\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{the longest increasing subsequence of
}w\text{ has length }\leq\ell\right.

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left.\text{and the longest decreasing subsequence of
}w\text{ has length }\leq k\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\text{ and }\lambda_{1}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}.

But the permutations counted on the left hand side are just the permutations
in
w\in S_{n}
that avoid
12\cdots\left(\ell+1\right)
and
\left(k+1\right)\ell\cdots 1
. Upon multiplying them by
w_{0}
from the left, they
become the permutations
w\in S_{n}
that avoid
12\cdots\left(k+1\right)

and
\left(\ell+1\right)\ell\cdots 1
(by Proposition

2.59
). Thus, the number of the latter permutations is

\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\text{ and }\lambda_{1}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
. This
proves Theorem
2.83
.

We may thus wonder:

Question 2.85
.

Can Theorem
2.83
be proved using
the ideals
\mathcal{I}_{k}
and
\mathcal{J}_{k}
?

One way to approach this is as follows. Let
T_{\operatorname*{sign}}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
be the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra automorphism
of
\mathcal{A}
sending each permutation
w\in S_{n}
to
\left(-1\right)^{w}w
. If
n!
is invertible in
\mathbf{k}
, then
\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\right)
is a free

\mathbf{k}
-submodule of
\mathcal{A}
having dimension
\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\text{
and }\lambda_{1}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}
. Thus, we could
answer Question
2.85
by finding a
\mathbf{k}
-module
basis of
\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\right)
indexed by permutations that avoid
12\cdots\left(k+1\right)
and
\left(\ell+1\right)\ell\cdots 1
. However, such a basis
cannot be independent on
\operatorname*{char}\mathbf{k}
like our above basis
of
\mathcal{I}_{k}
was, since (for instance) the subspace
\mathcal{I}_{2}\cap T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{2}\right)
for
n=3

has dimension
4
when
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Q}
but dimension
5
when

\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{F}_{2}
. While this does not strictly rule out a proof
using this subspace, it suggests that new methods are required, since the
techniques we have used to prove Theorem
2.17
above cannot
create a dependence on the characteristic of
\mathbf{k}
. The dimension of
the quotient
\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{J}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
can
depend on
\operatorname*{char}\mathbf{k}
as well (for
n=3
and
k=\ell=2
,
it equals
4
when
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{Q}
but equals
5
when
\mathbf{k}=\mathbb{F}_{2}
).

However, the “mixed” quotient

\mathcal{I}_{k}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)\cong\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)/T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)
behaves better.
Equivalently, the quotient
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
has a
basis of size
\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)
for all

\mathbf{k}
:

Theorem 2.86
.

Let
k,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the family
\left(\overline{w}\right)_{\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)}

is a basis of the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
.

We shall now outline a proof of this theorem (which might not be new; it is
probably a particular case of Donkin’s
[
Donkin24
, Corollary 2.12]
).
However, the proof itself relies on Theorem
2.83
, so it
produces no new proof of Theorem
2.83
. Thus, Question

2.85
remains open.

Proof 2.87

(Outline of the proof of Theorem
2.86
.)
.

The proof consists of two
steps:
(a)
showing that the family
\left(\overline{w}\right)_{\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)}
spans the

\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
, and

(b)
showing that this
\mathbf{k}
-module is free of rank
\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\right|
. Once these
two claims are proved, we will be easily able to conclude the proof using

[
Grinbe25
, Lemma 5.21.9]
.

We begin with step
(a)
. Thus, we set out to prove that the family

\left(\overline{w}\right)_{\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)}
spans the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
. In other words, we must prove that

\overline{u}\in\operatorname*{span}\left(\left(\overline{w}\right)_{\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)}\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for each }u\in S_{n}.

(76)

To prove this, we proceed by induction on
u
as in lexicographic order, as in
the proof of (
41
) above. Thus, we let
v\in S_{n}

be arbitrary, and we assume that (
76
) holds for all

u<v
; we must then prove that (
76
) holds for
u=v
. We
distinguish between three cases:

1.

If
v\in\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)
, then this claim
is obvious.

2.

If
v\in\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)
, then this
claim is proved just as in our above proof of (
41
), since
\mathcal{I}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)
.

3.

If
v\notin\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)
, then we proceed as follows: Set
v^{\prime}:=w_{0}v
. Then,

v\notin\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)

entails
v^{\prime}\notin\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(\ell+1\right)
(by Proposition
2.59
). Hence, the argument made in the
proof of (
44
) (but applied to
\ell
and

v^{\prime}
instead of
k
and
v
) shows that

\overline{v^{\prime}}=-\overline{\left(\text{some permutations }w>v^{\prime}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in }\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{J}_{\ell}.

Multiplying by
\overline{w_{0}}
on the left, we transform this into

\displaystyle\overline{w_{0}v^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=-\overline{\left(\text{some permutations
}w_{0}w\text{ with }w>v^{\prime}\right)}

\displaystyle=-\overline{\left(\text{some permutations }w^{\prime}<w_{0}v^{\prime}\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in }\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{J}_{\ell}

(because multiplying by
w_{0}
on the left flips the lexicographic order: if

x>y
in
S_{n}
, then
w_{0}x<w_{0}y
). Since
w_{0}v^{\prime}=v
(because

v^{\prime}=w_{0}v
but
w_{0}
is an involution), we can rewrite this as

\overline{v}=-\overline{\left(\text{some permutations }w^{\prime}<v\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in }\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{J}_{\ell}.

Applying the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra automorphism
T_{\operatorname*{sign}}
(or,
rather, the
\mathbf{k}
-algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}/T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)
it induces) to this equality, we obtain

\overline{v}=-\overline{\left(\text{some permutations }w^{\prime}<v\right)}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{in }\mathcal{A}/T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)

(we have suppressed the signs
\left(-1\right)^{v}
and
\left(-1\right)^{w^{\prime}}
here, since we are dealing with a linear combination
anyway). Thus, this equality also holds in
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
.
From here, we easily obtain (
76
) using the induction hypothesis.

Thus, (
76
) is proved, and we conclude that the family

\left(\overline{w}\right)_{\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)}
spans the
\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
.

Now we come to step
(b)
of our plan: showing that this
\mathbf{k}
-module is free of rank
\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\right|
.

Here we will need the Murphy cellular bases (see Remark
2.19
).
Namely, applying (
28
) to
\ell
instead of
k
, we
obtain
\mathcal{J}_{\ell}=\vphantom{\int_{g}^{g}}\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname*{std},\operatorname*{len}>\ell}^{-\operatorname*{Col}}
. Thus,

\displaystyle T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\vphantom{\int_{g}^{g}}\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname*{std},\operatorname*{len}>\ell}^{-\operatorname*{Col}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\nabla_{V,U}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\right)\ \mid\ \left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ and }\ell\left(\lambda\right)>\ell\right\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{V\mathbf{r},U\mathbf{r}}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\ \mid\ \left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ and }\ell\left(\lambda\right)>\ell\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, }T\mathbf{r}\text{ denotes the transpose of a tableau }T\text{,}\\
\text{and we have used }T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\nabla_{V,U}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\right)=\nabla_{V\mathbf{r},U\mathbf{r}}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\\
\text{(which is proved in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{sga}{}{}, Proposition 6.8.12]})}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{V,U}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\ \mid\ \left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ and }\ell\left(\lambda^{t}\right)>\ell\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{here, we have
substituted }\left(\lambda^{t},U\mathbf{r},V\mathbf{r}\right)\text{ for
}\left(\lambda,U,V\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{V,U}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\ \mid\ \left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ and }\lambda_{1}>\ell\right\}

(since
\ell\left(\lambda^{t}\right)=\lambda_{1}
for each partition

\lambda
). Adding this to

\mathcal{I}_{k}=\vphantom{\int_{g}^{g}}\mathcal{F}_{\operatorname*{std},\operatorname*{len}\leq k}^{\operatorname*{Row}}=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{V,U}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\ \mid\ \left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ and }\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\right\},

we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{V,U}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\ \mid\ \left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ and }\left(\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\text{ or }\lambda_{1}>\ell\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{V,U}^{\operatorname*{Row}}\ \mid\ \left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ and }\lambda\in X\right\},

where

X:=\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\text{ or
}\lambda_{1}>\ell\right\}.

This clearly shows that
\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)
is a direct addend of
\mathcal{A}
as a

\mathbf{k}
-module (since it is spanned by a subfamily of the row Murphy
basis), and is free of rank

\left(\text{\# of }\left(\lambda,U,V\right)\in\operatorname*{SBT}\left(n\right)\text{ such that }\lambda\in X\right)=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in X}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2},

whereas the quotient
\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
is
free of rank

\displaystyle\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\lambda\notin X\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)>k\text{ and }\lambda_{1}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}=\underbrace{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\lambda_{1}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(\ell+1\right)\right|\\
\text{(by Corollary
\ref{cor.num-avoid},}\\
\text{applied to }\ell\text{ instead of }k\text{)}\end{subarray}}-\underbrace{\sum\limits\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n;\\
\ell\left(\lambda\right)\leq k\text{ and }\lambda_{1}\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\left(f^{\lambda}\right)^{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\cap\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\right|\\
\text{(by Theorem \ref{thm.num-avoid2})}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(\ell+1\right)\right|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\right|\\
\text{(by
(\ref{pf.cor.row.twin.a.=}))}\end{subarray}}-\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\cap\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\right|-\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\cap\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\right|.

Finally, we are ready for everything to come together. We have shown that the

\mathbf{k}
-module
\mathcal{A}/\left(\mathcal{I}_{k}+T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell}\right)\right)
is free
of rank
\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\right|
. Hence,
[
Grinbe25
, Lemma 5.21.9]
shows that every family of

\left|\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)\right|

vectors that spans this
\mathbf{k}
-module must be a basis of this

\mathbf{k}
-module. Hence, the family
\left(\overline{w}\right)_{\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}^{\prime}\left(\ell+1\right)\setminus\operatorname*{Av}\nolimits_{n}\left(k+1\right)}
is a basis of
this
\mathbf{k}
-module (since we know that it spans it). This completes the
proof of Theorem
2.86
.

2.9.4
T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{n-k-1}\right)

after Artin–Wedderburn

We end with a brief excursion into some related work by Hamaker and Rhoades

[
HamRho25
]
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For any two
k
-tuples
\mathbf{a}=\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}\right)\in\left[n\right]^{k}
and
\mathbf{b}=\left(b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{k}\right)\in\left[n\right]^{k}
, we define the
element

\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}:=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in S_{n};\\
w\left(a_{i}\right)=b_{i}\text{ for all }i\end{subarray}}w\in\mathcal{A}.

(77)

Note that the
\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}
in Lemma
2.70
can thus be
rewritten as
\nabla_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}
. If we equip the set
\left[n\right]^{k}
with the left
S_{n}
-action given by

\displaystyle w\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}\right)

\displaystyle:=\left(w\left(a_{1}\right),w\left(a_{2}\right),\ldots,w\left(a_{k}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for all }w\in S_{n}\text{ and }\left(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{k}\right)\in\left[n\right]^{k}

(as we have already done in the proof of Theorem
2.67
), then we
can rewrite the definition (
77
) of
\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}
as

\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}:=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in S_{n};\\
w\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{b}\end{subarray}}w\in\mathcal{A}.

(78)

Thus,

\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}=0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{unless }\mathbf{b}=w\mathbf{a}\text{ for some }w\in S_{n}.

(79)

Now, Lemma
2.70
can be restated as follows:

Proposition 2.88
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{n-k-1}\right)=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\right\}.

Here, we understand
\mathcal{J}_{m}
to mean
\mathcal{A}
when
m<0
.

Proof 2.89
.

It is easy to see that
w\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}=\nabla_{w\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}
for any
w\in S_{n}
and any
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}
. Applying this to
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{a}
, we obtain

w\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}=\nabla_{w\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}

(80)

for any
w\in S_{n}
and any
\mathbf{a}\in\left[n\right]^{k}
(since

\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}=\nabla_{\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}
).

But Lemma
2.70
yields

\displaystyle T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{n-k-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathcal{A}}_{=\operatorname*{span}\left\{w\ \mid\ w\in S_{n}\right\}}\cdot\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\right\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{w\ \mid\ w\in S_{n}\right\}\cdot\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\right\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{w\nabla_{\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\text{ and }w\in S_{n}\right\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{w\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\text{ and }w\in S_{n}\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.prop.Nabbaspan.1})}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\text{ such that }\mathbf{b}=w\mathbf{a}\text{ for some }w\in S_{n}\right\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{eq.Nabba.0})}\right).

This proves Proposition
2.88
.

We conclude with the following coda to Theorem
2.75
:

Theorem 2.90
.

Assume that
n!
is invertible in
\mathbf{k}
. Let

k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let all notations be as in Theorem
2.75
. Then,

T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{n-k-1}\right)=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \lambda_{1}\geq n-k\right\}}.

Here,
\lambda_{1}
denotes the first entry of
\lambda
.

To prove this, we need a general fact about how
T_{\operatorname*{sign}}

interacts with the Artin–Wedderburn isomorphism
\operatorname*{AW}
:

Proposition 2.91
.

Assume that
n!
is invertible in
\mathbf{k}
. Let all
notations be as in Theorem
2.75
. Let
X
be a subset of
\left\{\lambda\vdash n\right\}
. Let
\lambda^{t}
denote the transpose (i.e.,
conjugate) of any partition
\lambda
. Then,

T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{X}\right)=\mathcal{A}_{X^{t}},

where
X^{t}
denotes the subset
\left\{\lambda^{t}\ \mid\ \lambda\in X\right\}=\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \lambda^{t}\in X\right\}
of

\left\{\lambda\vdash n\right\}
.

Proof 2.92

(Proof of Proposition
2.91
.)
.

Let
\lambda
be a partition of

n
. It is well-known (e.g.,
[
Grinbe25
, Theorem 5.18.13]
) that the sign-twist
of the Specht module
S^{\lambda}
satisfies
\left(S^{\lambda}\right)^{\operatorname*{sign}}\cong S^{\lambda^{t}}
. Thus, in turn,
S^{\lambda}\cong\left(S^{\lambda^{t}}\right)^{\operatorname*{sign}}
(since each

S_{n}
-representation
V
satisfies
\left(V^{\operatorname*{sign}}\right)^{\operatorname*{sign}}=V
). Hence, for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we have the
equivalence

\displaystyle\left(aS^{\lambda}=0\right)\

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(a\left(S^{\lambda^{t}}\right)^{\operatorname*{sign}}=0\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\ \left(T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(a\right)S^{\lambda^{t}}=0\right)

(81)

(because the definition of
\left(S^{\lambda^{t}}\right)^{\operatorname*{sign}}
reveals that
a\left(S^{\lambda^{t}}\right)^{\operatorname*{sign}}=T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(a\right)S^{\lambda^{t}}
as
\mathbf{k}
-modules). Now, (
74
)
yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{X}

\displaystyle=\left\{a\in\mathcal{A}\ \mid\ aS^{\lambda}=0\text{ for
all }\lambda\notin X\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{a\in\mathcal{A}\ \mid\ T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(a\right)S^{\lambda^{t}}=0\text{ for all }\lambda\notin X\right\}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\text{by (\ref{pf.prop.AW.dual-A.equiv})}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{a\in\mathcal{A}\ \mid\ T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(a\right)S^{\lambda}=0\text{ for all }\lambda\notin X^{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{here, we have substituted }\lambda\text{ for }\lambda^{t}\text{,}\\
\text{since }X^{t}=\left\{\lambda^{t}\ \mid\ \lambda\in X\right\}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=T_{\operatorname*{sign}}^{-1}\left(\underbrace{\left\{a\in\mathcal{A}\ \mid\ aS^{\lambda}=0\text{ for all }\lambda\notin X^{t}\right\}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathcal{A}_{X^{t}}\\
\text{(by (\ref{pf.prop.IJ.rep.AU=}),
applied to }U=X^{t}\text{)}\end{subarray}}\right)

\displaystyle=T_{\operatorname*{sign}}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{A}_{X^{t}}\right).

Since
T_{\operatorname*{sign}}
is an isomorphism, we thus conclude that

T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{X}\right)=\mathcal{A}_{X^{t}}
. This proves Proposition
2.91
.

Proof 2.93

(Proof of Theorem
2.90
.)
.

The case
k\geq n
is left to the reader
(both sides are
0
). In the case
k<n
, we have
n-k-1\in\mathbb{N}
, so that
we can apply Theorem
2.75
to
n-k-1
instead of
k
. Thus, from
the last equality of Theorem
2.75
, we find

\mathcal{J}_{n-k-1}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)>n-k-1\right\}}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\geq n-k\right\}}.

(82)

Now, let us use the notations of Proposition
2.91
. Applying
Proposition
2.91
to
X=\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\geq n-k\right\}
, we obtain

\displaystyle T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\geq n-k\right\}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda\right)\geq n-k\right\}^{t}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \ell\left(\lambda^{t}\right)\geq n-k\right\}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(\begin{array}[c]{c}\text{since }X^{t}=\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \lambda^{t}\in X\right\}\\
\text{for each }X\subseteq\left\{\lambda\vdash n\right\}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \lambda_{1}\geq n-k\right\}},

because it is well-known (see, e.g.,
[
Grinbe25
, Theorem 5.1.10
(c)
]
)
that every partition
\lambda
satisfies
\ell\left(\lambda^{t}\right)=\lambda_{1}
. In light of (
82
), we can rewrite this as

T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\mathcal{J}_{n-k-1}\right)=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \lambda_{1}\geq n-k\right\}}.

Theorem
2.90
is thus proved.

Combining Theorem
2.90
with Proposition
2.88
, we
conclude that

\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\right\}=\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \lambda_{1}\geq n-k\right\}}

(83)

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, under the assumption that
n!
is invertible in

\mathbf{k}
. This is essentially
[
HamRho25
, Theorem 3.5]
.
(“Essentially” because the

\operatorname*{Loc}\nolimits_{k}\left(\mathfrak{S}_{n},\mathbb{C}\right)

from
[
HamRho25
]
is not
\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\right\}
but rather
\operatorname*{span}\left\{\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}\ \mid\ \mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}\text{
injective}\right\}
, where a
k
-tuple is said to be
injective
if its

k
entries are distinct.) The same result appears in
[
ElFrPi11
, Theorem
7]
(where, again, the “
k
-cosets” are the
\nabla_{\mathbf{b},\mathbf{a}}
for injective
k
-tuples

\mathbf{a},\mathbf{b}\in\left[n\right]^{k}
). Note that the condition
“
\lambda_{1}\geq n-k
” on a partition

\lambda
of
n
is equivalent to “
\lambda\geq\left(n-k,1^{k}\right)
in lexicographic order”, and this is the
condition used in
[
ElFrPi11
, Theorem 7]
.

It is perhaps also worth mentioning that
[
EveZoh20
, Lemma 3]
is saying
that if
\mathbf{k}
is a field of characteristic
0
, and if
l\in\left[n\right]
, then

\mathcal{A}_{\left\{\lambda\vdash n\ \mid\ \lambda_{1}<l\right\}}=\left(\mathcal{A}\nabla_{\left[l\right]}\mathcal{A}\right)^{\perp},

(84)

where
\nabla_{\left[l\right]}
is the sum of all permutations
w\in S_{n}
that fix the elements
l+1,l+2,\ldots,n
. This can be derived from
Theorem
2.75
, Theorem
2.17

(a)
and
Proposition
2.14

(f)
using Proposition

2.91
(and the fact that
\nabla_{\left[l\right]}=T_{\operatorname*{sign}}\left(\nabla_{\left[l\right]}^{-}\right)
).
We leave the details to the reader.
